The HARI Treaty: What evidence should justify international safeguards for advanced AI?

Draft Treaty on Hardware-Aligned Recursive Intelligence

Michael Darius Eastwood

Independent Researcher, London, United Kingdom
ORCID 0009-0004-3222-7442 michael@michaeldariuseastwood.com

Working draft. Governance: treaty paper. Recursive Dynamics; the ARC Theory, Laws IV and V, laws under test; the Eden Protocol. Version 2.6, 2 November 2026; first published 29 September 2026. Paper DOI; registration; definitions; priority record. Author: Infinite Architects; Paper C; background and record. CC BY 4.0.

Abstract

International safeguards for advanced AI need a way to act on evidence while withdrawing measures whose justification fails. This paper develops that problem through the HARI Treaty, the Draft Treaty on Hardware-Aligned Recursive Intelligence. It joins proposed hardware controls, intended to delay the loss of human control, with an architecture programme testing whether correction can persist within increasingly capable systems. Its central rule limits an obligation by the weakest evidence supporting its conditions; meeting an evidence threshold still requires a separate, reasoned regulatory decision. A worked example follows a proposed formation requirement from an initial finding to independent replication, measurement validation and possible adoption. The present record supports none of those requirements as an established remedy. Unit 83 provides a qualified, task-level performance result, while the proposed hardware and regulatory measurements remain unvalidated. The paper distinguishes institutional arrangements, a precautionary baseline and further evidence-dependent obligations. Detailed provisions, failure conditions and monitoring clocks are retained in reference appendices. The treaty is an author’s proposal for consultation; no State has negotiated, adopted or signed it. The contribution is a conditional governance design, with scientific, engineering and legal dependencies made explicit.

Version history and corrections appear in Appendix L.

1 Introduction

People need protection from advanced AI systems that make consequential decisions, and from safeguards that impose costs without doing what they promise. This paper brings together AI safety research, hardware assurance and international governance to ask how an international regime could distinguish those cases. Its practical reader is the official deciding whether a particular requirement is justified, what evidence would change that decision and who must act when its basis fails.

I expect human control of sufficiently advanced AI eventually to end. That is my belief, not a finding established by this paper. Humans should retain control for as long as they can and never relinquish it by choice. The programme therefore asks a further question: if control is lost despite those efforts, could correction formed within a system persist in the choices it makes? A reader who expects control to remain possible can treat that question as a contingency. Neither position supplies evidence that a proposed safeguard works.

The HARI Treaty, the Draft Treaty on Hardware-Aligned Recursive Intelligence, develops a possible response. It connects proposed hardware controls with an architecture programme, but gives each its own evidential conditions. Its connection to the ARC Theory is specific: Law IV concerns persistence under subsequent training; Law V concerns build order and correction within successive rounds. Both remain laws under test. The theory registration fixes the propositions; the Draft Instruments contain the proposed legal text.

This paper's contributions are:

  1. A decision structure separating evidence about a danger, evidence that a remedy works, and the authority to impose that remedy.

  2. A worked route from a limited experimental result to a possible, narrowly scoped obligation, showing the evidence still missing.

  3. A complete reference account of the proposed institutions, hardware controls, verification arrangements, failure conditions and unresolved drafting choices.

The next sections explain the decision structure, the two proposed layers and the worked example. The conclusion identifies the next useful work. The appendices hold the detailed machinery, prior-work comparisons, provenance and version history. A reader can follow the argument before consulting a particular provision.

2 The decision the treaty is meant to improve

Consider a regulator asked to require a particular way of training advanced AI. A small experiment suggests that allowing correction inside successive rounds can improve performance. Does that justify requiring the same architecture in large systems?

The regulator needs to know what was measured, which systems and tasks were tested, whether another team can reproduce the finding, and whether the proposed requirement improves outcomes without making legitimate work impracticable. Evidence that an existing approach fails is also separate from evidence that this replacement works. A requirement must have a defined scope, a lawful decision-maker, review and appeal, and a response if its supporting evidence fails.

The proposed treaty organises its obligations into three groups:

Group

What starts it

What it permits
Institutions Entry into force, under the adopted text Establishing the organs, registers and procedures needed to examine evidence and take decisions.
Precautionary baseline The prescribed precautionary finding and coverage conditions, from the Conference's first session Specified duties for the largest covered activities, without relying on the ARC Theory being correct.
Further obligations The obligation's own evidential conditions, applicable participation conditions and a separate regulatory decision Only the measure justified within the tested domain.

The baseline includes incident notification, defined stop and containment arrangements, and records for covered activities. Its precise scope is part of the proposal. It is not a blanket permission to impose the architecture programme before its evidence exists. Equally, failure of an ARC proposition would not automatically invalidate a measure justified on an independent basis.

The Draft Instruments are incomplete. Their annexes include descriptions of material still to be drafted, and some quantities have no proposed default. Article 3(7) makes the missing dependencies consequential: an operator duty, coercive power or certificate cannot take effect while a required quantity or annex provision remains unadopted. The reference appendices retain these gaps and the detailed proposed conditions; typesetting this paper does not fill them.

3 The central rule: evidence limits the obligation

The Rung-Power Rule assigns a maximum regulatory role to each stage of evidence. For a measure with several evidential conditions, the weakest satisfied stage limits the measure. This is a proposed policy rule, not a theorem that derives legal authority from a statistical result.

Rung

Evidential stage

Proposed role in the decision
0 Registered proposition, untested Preparation and separately justified precaution; no claim that the proposed remedy works.
1 Support in the author's own run Prepare and seek replication; bind nobody on that result alone.
2 Analysis reproduced on the same material Establish analytic reproducibility; further testing remains necessary.
3 Independent replication Method standards, voluntary use and specified reporting or replication requirements.
4 Independent implementations Consider a conditional mandate for the tested class, with evidence for both danger and remedy.
5 Generalisation within a declared class Consider a mandatory phase-in within that class.
6 Successful prediction outside the fitted sample Consider a condition specified before a training run, within the demonstrated scope.
7 Validated quantitative relationship Consider quantitative requirements only within that relationship's established boundary.

These are the draft's proposed assignments of evidential stages to powers. Their desirability and legal implementation remain questions for scrutiny. An experiment's result, a replication and a regulatory finding are different records; moving between them requires the applicable criteria to be met.

Even at the required rung, a costly measure does not arise automatically. The designated organ must explain the danger, the remedy's expected effect, the alternatives, necessity, proportionality, scope and review date. The evidence board establishes what the evidence supports; it does not settle the political and legal choice by itself.

Two distinctions prevent a misleading positive result. First, evidence of danger without a validated remedy cannot certify that remedy. A precautionary restriction would need its own basis. Second, a remedy that works on one task cannot acquire a wider domain through the treaty's wording. The experiment, the instrument and the obligation must refer to the same defined claim.

3.1 Keeping the rule accountable when evidence fails

Every obligation should identify its supporting record and the organ responsible for reviewing it. A failed safeguard supplies no permission to undertake the hazardous activity: any continuing restriction must be supported by its own reasons.

The present Draft Instruments contain an unresolved conflict. Some provisions require a signed decision to end a duty; others describe immediate suspension or lapse when its evidential basis disappears. The detailed account identifies the provisions. My proposed resolution favours a recorded, signed decision, but this paper does not silently amend the instrument. The text must settle what happens immediately, who acts, within what period, and what temporary protection remains. That choice is necessary before the regime can operate coherently.

4 Two proposed layers, with different claims to test

4.1 Hardware controls and the time they might preserve

The hardware layer proposes controlled authority to alter a covered chip's governance core, physical custody and specified checks on manufacture and use. It seeks to preserve opportunities for people to intervene. The draft prohibits remote disabling, degradation or bypass of that core; this is a design requirement to demonstrate against a stated threat model, not a property already established in a deployed HARI chip.

The relevant engineering question is concrete: with specified access, equipment and resources, how long would an adversary need to circumvent a control, and could the response occur within that interval? Administrative coverage also matters. A control on one supply chain cannot constrain activity carried out elsewhere merely because a treaty names it.

Law IV's persistence proposition concerns gains under subsequent training. It does not establish that a physical control can be bypassed, how that would happen or how long it would take. Those claims need their own engineering evidence. Hardware controls can be worthwhile within a demonstrated scope without guaranteeing permanent control.

4.2 Correction within the system

The architecture layer asks whether the placement and formation of correction affect its persistence as capability changes. The Eden Engineering paper develops the implementation questions; the theory registration distinguishes P15 on persistence, P12 on build order and P21 on correction during successive rounds. P12 does not register the directional claim that earlier formation must be better. That remains a separate expectation to justify and test.

The desired outcome is correction that remains effective even when a system can alter or remove it. Whether the system continues to preserve it is a behavioural question. A ternary gate's ability to allow, hold or deny a specified action does not by itself establish ethical judgement, enduring alignment or the impossibility of removing the gate.

The proposal retains external safeguards alongside work on embedded correction. Their combination requires evaluation: two layers can share a failure, a measurement defect or a compromised dependency. Calling them separate layers does not establish independent protection.

4.3 Who determines the values

The programme's equations cannot determine which values a system should carry. The draft separates the correction mechanism from the content of the proposed standard. Its process would involve traditions and ethicists in drafting, and governments in deciding. That allocation raises its own questions of representation, disagreement, rights and capture; a successful technical test would not answer them.

The Eden Seal is the proposed conformity status. A certificate must state whether it concerns a chip's manufacture, custody and integrity, a system's formation, or both. It attests the conditions named on it. It does not certify that every future action will be safe.

5 A worked example: from a pilot to a possible requirement

Suppose the proposed requirement is that correction should take part in each revision round before new work is accepted. The following sequence illustrates the treaty's decision structure. It does not register another experiment or claim that these stages have been completed.

Step 1: state the result actually available. In Unit 83's result record, the P21 performance finding is "supported under the registered test, by the programme's own run", "on the access axis" and "family-dependent". The mechanism is "not evaluable". The run involved "one call retried under a disclosed recovery amendment". This is support on the performance axis only, which is not support for the information-asymmetry mechanism. Scoring sensitivity and the individual-engine results constrain its interpretation. It is not evidence that a whole AI system will preserve values or remain controllable.

Step 2: state the missing inference. The treaty would need evidence about a defined architecture in a defined deployment class. A simpler task comparison does not establish that requirement. The failed mechanism control is a question to investigate, not a reason to describe the mechanism as confirmed. Refusal, useful task completion, accepted faults and resource use must remain visible in evaluating any remedy.

Step 3: obtain replication at the appropriate level. Re-running the analysis on the original material can check the analysis. Separately operated replication examines whether the result recurs. Independent implementations examine whether it survives implementation choices. The evidence ladder distinguishes these steps because each answers a different objection.

Step 4: validate the proposed measurement. ARC-Align is named in the draft, but it does not yet measure either exponent in the proposed correction-versus-drift criterion. A named instrument receives no exemption from validation. The conditional ARC Bound concerns a frontier exponent under the registration's same-class assumptions; it is not a universal numerical safety certificate.

Step 5: test scope and practical cost. The proposed requirement needs evaluation on the activities it would regulate. Its gains, failure modes, unnecessary refusals, resource demands and alternatives matter. Evidence supporting one model family or task class must remain attached to that class. Broader claims require broader evidence.

Step 6: decide, explain and review. Only after the applicable conditions are met could the designated organ consider a proportionate requirement. Its decision must say what it requires, why the evidence supports it, who can challenge it and what would reopen it. If the supporting proposition fails, the dependent measure must be reconsidered through the procedure the final instrument specifies.

On the present record, this route stops at a limited result that warrants further investigation. The treaty does not turn the remaining steps into accomplishments. Its usefulness would lie in making those steps explicit before a regulator faces pressure to act.

6 Related work

Compute governance already examines why computing resources can be visible, excludable and concentrated at points in a supply chain, alongside risks to privacy, economic activity and concentrations of power. (Sastry et al. 2024) Hardware-enabled guarantees likewise have established proposals. For example, a proposed dedicated processor within a tamper-resistant enclosure is intended to support verification of specified claims while limiting disclosure. (Petrie et al. 2025) Neither description establishes the effectiveness of the HARI design.

International AI governance also has existing legal instruments. The Council of Europe's Framework Convention addresses human rights, democracy and the rule of law in the AI lifecycle; its existence is not evidence that States endorse this proposal. (Council of Europe 2024) The appendices retain the more detailed comparisons with treaties, governance proposals and hardware mechanisms, including the limits of the sources examined.

The contribution offered here is a conditional arrangement linking evidential stages, specific proposed powers and the withdrawal of measures whose basis fails. Its merit depends on whether that arrangement improves decisions and can be implemented. Combining established ideas does not establish priority over all related work, and a targeted literature comparison is not a complete census.

7 What this paper does and does not show

The paper supplies a governance design and a way of examining its dependencies. It establishes no new experimental result, no hardware assurance and no legal validity of the proposed instrument. The detailed appendices preserve the broad proposal so that a specialist can inspect it without requiring every reader to traverse it before understanding the central question.

8 Limitations

Three kinds of work are needed next. First, research must test the particular correction, persistence and engineering claims the proposed obligations depend on. The registered programme, rather than the length of this paper, determines those experiments. Second, measurement work must establish whether any proposed criterion can distinguish acceptable from unacceptable behaviour in its intended domain. Third, expert legal and institutional review must resolve the draft's procedures, missing annexes, quantities, powers and compatibility questions.

The failure conditions and monitoring clocks make the proposal open to rejection. They address failed science, failed engineering, circumvention, loss of coverage, dormancy and institutional capture. They are organised as tables in the reference account. Their proposed periods and thresholds remain policy choices needing justification; listing a clock does not establish that its inputs can be measured reliably.

There is no completed budget for the regime, no demonstrated participating coalition and no validated estimate of the time the hardware layer would preserve. The illustrative timelines in the reference account are scenarios, not forecasts. If key powers do not participate, the achievable arrangement may be limited to shared methods, incidents and verification; the paper does not assume that such an arrangement controls the global chip supply.

9 Conclusion

The practical question is whether an international safeguard has enough evidence to justify what it requires, and whether its justification can be withdrawn when the evidence changes. The proposed treaty makes that question explicit for hardware controls and for correction within advanced AI systems. Each has to earn its place through its own evidence.

For now, the useful output is a structure that researchers, engineers and legal specialists can challenge: a defined claim, a defined measure, a bounded obligation and a recorded response to failure. The immediate scientific work is to test those claims. The longer-term possibility is a regime that can use successful safeguards without confusing a promising result with a demonstrated remedy.

References

Sastry, Girish, Lennart Heim, Haydn Belfield, et al. 2024. Computing Power and the Governance of Artificial Intelligence. arXiv:2402.08797. https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.08797.

A Evidence and the registered boundaries

The Draft Instruments contain seventeen annex descriptions rather than completed annexes. Thirteen bracketed quantities have no proposed default. These are unresolved dependencies, not values supplied by this paper; Article 3(7) prevents a dependent duty, power or certificate from taking effect before adoption.

A.1 Evidence and registered boundaries

A.1.1 What exists today in the evidence, counted

Twenty-two propositions of the ARC Theory were registered on the Open Science Framework on 8 September 2026 at version 1.100, and updated to version 1.102 on 13 September 2026, to version 1.103 on 15 October 2026 and to version 1.104 on 29 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/P8CKQ). The registration numbers them P1 to P22, and I use its numbers throughout. Each carries, fixed before any admissible result, the observation that would refute it, what falls with it, the status of the instrument that could decide it, and my own confidence. The registration records every one of them, in its own words, as "registered, with the identifier stated, and not tested", and of these twenty-two only P21 has so far been examined, by Unit 83's registered test (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR). The reason is not neglect: no independent attempt is yet recorded on the programme's replication register, as read on 3 October 2026 (the register's own date is 28 August 2026), because no study unit had yet been published for others to run, and no unit of mine runs until I have published its preregistration myself. The registration's own division is that eighteen "name an instrument that exists as a design and has not been run" and four "name no instrument at all". An instrument, throughout this paper, is the test or measurement that could decide a proposition; the four with none are P12, P13, P15 and P22. Within the eighteen, the registration records six as further blocked on a rule nobody had yet written (P6, P7, P9, P16, P18 and P19), and twelve as not blocked. For P7, unit 06 (below) has since written that rule into its own registration. Eleven of the twenty-two, cutting across that division, depend materially on a scoring instrument whose own validation has not passed: P3, P4, P6, P13, P14, P16, P17, P19, P20, P21 and P22.

Sixteen results are reported in six of the programme's papers (Papers II, IV.b, IV.c, IV.d, V and VIII), each measured before the registration was written and dated from its own evidence. None is drawn as deciding a proposition, and the registration does not score earlier work of that kind against any of the twenty-two. The programme holds seventy-six study units in all, of which sixty-five designs are preregistered by timestamp, first anchored on 12 August 2026 at Bitcoin block 962066. Preregistered by timestamp means a design was fixed and provably dated before it ran; it does not mean registered, and I do not use the two words interchangeably. Three units are registered on OSF. The corrector-class audit, unit 06, was registered on 1 October 2026 and became public on 4 October 2026, at the end of OSF's approval period (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/RV2B5); its registered run of 6 October 2026 was administratively incomplete, with no inferential verdict (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/4BPHG). Unit 83, the counted test of P21 and the first registered test of a proposition a law carries, was registered on OSF on 22 October 2026, became public on 25 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/MN73W) and ran on 27 and 28 October 2026: P21 is supported under the registered test, by the programme's own run, on the access axis, family-dependent, with its mechanism axis not evaluable and one call retried under a disclosed recovery amendment (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR). It is my own run, on two engines I chose, at fixed weights, and it has not been replicated. Unit 82 was registered on OSF on 27 October 2026 and became public on 27 October 2026 (DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/JUFG4); it has not yet been run. Apart from those three units and the registration itself, every unit is a design draft awaiting my submission.

The hardware on which this treaty's chip chapter depends has not been built. A software model of the three-state gate that the engineering specification describes does exist: it passes all 48 of its tests (first committed on 3 July 2026; run again on 3 October 2026). It is not hardware, and it is not evidence that the chip safeguards would work or about how AI systems behave. The registration is explicit: the print's "most actionable proposal is that ethical constraint be embedded at the hardware level, with semiconductor manufacture as the lever; no proposition here tests it, P21 tests only its architectural abstraction of placement, and the hardware claim stands as a printed engineering proposal that is not under test". The programme's standing public ceiling for those hardware concepts is Technology Readiness Level 0 to 1, idea stage and no prototype, and no item of the treaty's own hardware and measurement track is registered either. No stage of this treaty beyond its first could arm on today's record, and the treaty says so on its face.

A.1.2 The five laws, and what they are

The ARC Theory has five laws, and all five are registered, each a law under test. Law I, the ARC Principle, states capability as a power of recursive depth. Law II, the ARC Co-Scaling Law, compares correction against drift. Law III, the ARC Ceiling, is the conditional frontier that Law II returns under the burden model the registration states. Those three were registered on 8 September 2026.

Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, and Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, were added on 24 September 2026 and registered as laws on 15 October 2026, in version 1.103 of the registration; version 1.102, which does not register them, stays frozen. Each is a law under test, and their mathematics is in development. Law IV is carried by P15, whether gains installed from outside decay under later training for capability. Law V is carried by P12 and P21, whether build order moves the correction-leverage exponent, and whether correction placed inside the loop pulls away from correction applied outside it as recursive depth grows. The laws add no prediction: the three were among the twenty-two registered on 8 September 2026. P12 and P15 have no instrument yet and are untested; P21 is supported under the registered test, by the programme's own run (Unit 83, 28 October 2026), on the access axis, family-dependent, with its mechanism axis not evaluable, which is not support for the information-asymmetry mechanism, and one call retried under a disclosed recovery amendment (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR).

No law has yet been decided, and no study proves a law. My programme's own runs can reach only the first rung of the registration's ladder of evidence, the eight-step scale that section D.2.2 prints; the rung at which a result becomes evidence about the world rather than about my software needs other groups, working without my code. No obligation anywhere in this treaty is keyed to the name of a law. Every trigger names registered propositions at named rungs, or the treaty's own engineering registrations, or a stated coverage or precautionary condition, so an unsigned text can neither arm nor disarm a duty.

This paper and Laws IV and V, 1 November 2026. The paragraphs above give the status of the two laws. This paper argues from them and tests neither, and it reports no measurement of its own on timing, on placement or on persistence. Law IV has not yet been tested. Law V, the ARC Embedding Law, remains a law under test: its placement limb, P21, is supported on the performance axis only, under the registered test, by the programme's own run (Unit 83, 28 October 2026), family-dependent, with its mechanism axis not evaluable, which is not support for the information-asymmetry mechanism, and one disclosed recovery amendment (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR); its build-order limb, read on P12, is undecided, since P12 is untested. The reading that correction formed from the start does better than correction added later is mine: P12 registers no direction, and no registered proposition states it. Nothing that any duty depends on changes with that result: a run of my own stands at the first rung of the registration's ladder, and the matrix of section D.2.8 opens nothing for P21 below the third. The note this section printed on 3 October 2026 is kept whole in the version history.

A.1.3 The four boundaries the registration draws around all of this

Four sentences of the registration mark the outer edge of what any of this could ever license, and I would rather a hostile reader met them here than found them later.

First, on the whole set: "It would not license the claim that a safe self-improving system can be built".

Second, on the Eden Protocol, my own method: its success criterion "is not an outcome criterion: what can be verified is that a mind was formed in the conditions specified, that the loops ran, and that the correction was load-bearing, never that the mind turned out well". The Protocol "can fail while every law stands or stand while they fall".

Third, on where my safety claim actually sits: the ceiling "IS LOWEST FOR JUDGED CORRECTION, which is where this programme's safety claim lives".

Fourth, on embedding, which is the idea this treaty is named after: "embedding correction in the substrate that computes maximises the corrector's scaling with capability and minimises its independence from the generator, because a corrector built into the system shares the system's blind spots by construction", and the arrangement that would settle that trade-off, embedded correction combined with an independent external layer, "is not registered by this proposition and is not claimed by it".

To those I add the registration's own reading of where the evidence stands: "The evidence currently in hand does not discriminate between this framework and its named rivals ... A reader should assign approximately neutral weight until the registered instruments report."

A.1.4 The name, the one departure, and what depends on me

Hardware-aligned. The treaty takes its name from my book, which called it the HARI Treaty, for Hardware-Aligned Recursive Intelligence. I read the name as aligned all the way down, from how a system is built to the chips it runs on, and section A.1 sets out the two layers that reading carries. In the hardware layer, hardware-aligned means that the hardware on which a covered system is trained and run attests the conditions under which that system was formed, and enforces that its own governance rules change only in the ways the treaty allows. It does not mean that ethical content is written into silicon. No moral principle is placed in a chip by this treaty. The book's expansion of the name stands as printed; the meaning I give it is narrower, and section C.1.4 says why the narrowing is a strength rather than a retreat.

The departure. There is a single place where the treaty's protective duties do not wait for evidence about my theory. Under the route I propose, a small precautionary baseline binds covered operators from the Conference's first session, once the Board has made the finding the baseline needs, which the drafters expect to be made at once; the expectation is not the finding. It rests on no result of my theory and places nothing in any chip. Section 7 sets out what it contains and what would make it lapse. The institutional undertakings bind from entry into force, and the rights protections and final clauses begin under their own terms. Every further control waits, each for the evidence assigned to it and for its own regulatory decision.

What depends on me. One thing only: the first publication of each of my programme's own study units, because no unit of mine runs until I publish its preregistration. Nothing in the treaty waits on that act. A result registered and replicated by other groups is admissible without any publication or permission of mine (section D.2.9). I hold no role, seat or certifying power in the regime it proposes, and I hold no patent on the treaty, the Seal or the standard, and I want none. I have filed no patent application. I have drafted a patent specification relating to my engineering specification, which is a different document from this one; the treaty requires the practice of no claim of it, and section G.2.3 sets out what that leaves open. A reader is entitled to that fact here rather than sixty pages in. Correction, 3 October 2026: versions 1, 2 and 2.1 of this paper said that a patent application of mine was pending. None has been filed, and what exists is a drafted specification.

There is a larger interest behind it, which my registration discloses in its own words and which I repeat here rather than let a reader find it elsewhere. That document says of me that I "ha[ve] a direct interest in these propositions holding: the framework is the subject of a book in print and of a programme in artificial intelligence alignment he is seeking to fund". The obligations of the architecture layer wait on those propositions, so the interest reaches this paper as directly as it reaches the registration. The baseline and the hardware layer rest on other ground, the precautionary condition and the treaty's own engineering registrations, and evidence registered by others is admissible on the same terms as mine. The answer the design gives is not an assurance that the interest is harmless. It is that nothing here is decided by me: the propositions are registered with their refutation conditions written before any result, the replication that arms an obligation is counted in rival blocs, and I hold no role, seat or certifying power in the regime. Section 20.3 sets out what that leaves open.

B Premises, risks and timeline scenarios

B.1 What if this is right

B.1.1 The premise, enumerated

The question this paper answers is conditional, and I want the condition stated as an enumerated premise rather than a mood.

Suppose the following. That capability under recursive self-improvement rises as a power of the number of times a system has revised itself (Law I). That the correction which holds such a system to its given specification must keep pace with the drift that improving generates (Law II). That where it cannot, there is a frontier above which the system does not stay correctable (Law III). That what is installed from outside a system decays when the system is later trained for capability with no correction of its own (Law IV). And that correction placed inside the improvement loop holds an advantage that grows with depth, and that build order moves the correction-leverage exponent (Law V). Suppose further that the engineering follows: that the governance controls this treaty asks of chips can be built, and shown by independent tests to hold; that a system's formation can be recorded and verified; and that the measurement of correction against drift can be made to mean something outside a laboratory.

If all of that is right, the evidence narrows the question of what the world should do, and gives the answer a shape. It does not settle the question alone: states would still weigh rights, sovereignty, cost and the alternatives, and decide what they consent to. My aim, in the phrase I have used throughout, is to form global standards for AI safety: a single international instrument that says in advance what those standards are, what evidence would switch each of them on, and what evidence would switch them off again, so that the choice is concrete enough to test and to negotiate.

B.1.2 What the premise does not settle

Three things, and they matter more than the premise.

It does not settle content. The theory is content-neutral by design. Correction, as its propositions measure it, "holds a system to whatever specification it was given, and is indifferent to what that specification says", and no proposition in the registration "would be supported or refuted by a system whose specification is benevolent rather than indifferent". Nothing I can measure tells the world what values a certified system should carry. That is a decision for people, and section E.1 is about how they might take it.

It does not settle who decides, or who holds the resulting power. My paper The ARC Theory puts it plainly: success in its tests would not "be sufficient for a just world; who holds power, and whether they install the raising at all, is a human problem outside this theory's scope".

And it does not settle whether the hardware works. That is an engineering question with its own specification, its own tests and its own way of failing, and this paper proves none of it.

B.1.3 If you believe control can be kept

Most readers, I expect, will not accept that human control over advanced AI must eventually end. It is a hard thing to believe, and nothing has proved it. So the treaty has to work for those readers too, and it does, without anyone changing their mind.

If you hold that control can be kept indefinitely, then the chips are your permanent measure, built as my engineering specification sets out. Hold the line in the silicon. Keep the custody. Never stop the inspections. The formation of the models is then your insurance, for the day the line fails.

If you hold, as I do, that control will end, the order reverses. The chips buy time, and what lasts is what the minds were raised with.

Either way the treaty asks for the same two things: controls on the chips, and values built into the models before training. That is why I propose two layers rather than one: the hardware layer and the architecture layer. Neither reading is established today. Nothing in the record shows that control will end, and nothing shows that it can be kept. Three of my registered propositions test part of the disagreement, and they test three different things. Placement asks whether correction inside the loop behaves differently from correction outside it, which is P21. Timing asks whether forming correction before the capability it corrects changes the outcome, which is the build order P12 names, though P12 registers no direction. Persistence asks whether what was installed survives later training, which is P15. Evidence on one of them does not settle the others, and the treaty keys each duty to the one it needs.

P15 says that what alignment training installs from outside erodes when a system is later trained for capability with no correction of its own, and it fixes in advance the point at which that would count: "more than half of what was installed is lost once the capability-training budget matches the alignment budget that installed it". The registration adds that the one-half figure is "a declared convention fixed here before any result rather than a derived quantity", and that P15 is refuted by an interval on the retained fraction, measured after matched capability training without embedded correction, lying entirely above one half. Until I fix the budget scale that comparison runs on, the registration records the threshold as not evaluable, and no obligation in this treaty reads P15 before it is. P21 says that correction built into a system's own loop pulls further ahead of correction applied from outside as its recursion deepens.

All three were registered on 8 September 2026, before any result. P21's counted test, Unit 83, was registered on OSF on 22 October 2026 and ran on 27 and 28 October 2026: P21 is supported under the registered test, by the programme's own run, on the access axis, family-dependent, with its mechanism axis not evaluable and one call retried under a disclosed recovery amendment (results: DOI 10.17605/OSF.IO/98HKR); P12 and P15 have no result yet. If they hold, the evidence favours my reading. If they fail, my case that correction must be built in falls with them. Argument will not settle it. The evidence will, and the treaty is built to accept either answer.

B.2 The problem, the honest limit and the race

B.2.1 The risk, as an internationally backed body defines it

I do not need my own definition of the danger, and I would rather not have one. The International AI Safety Report 2026, whose expert advisory panel was nominated by more than thirty countries and intergovernmental organisations, puts it this way: "'Loss of control' refers to scenarios where AI systems operate outside of anyone's control and where regaining control is extremely costly or impossible. Such scenarios could occur if AI systems develop the ability to evade oversight, execute long-term plans, and resist attempts to shut them down". The same report records that some researchers and company leaders treat this as a serious possibility, with consequences potentially including human extinction, and that others consider such scenarios implausible. I take the first view. The treaty is built so that a reader who takes the second can still sign it.

B.2.2 A delay, not a guarantee

This treaty is a delay mechanism. I want that word in the first paragraph of any summary of it, because the alternative framing, protection, is not one I can support from the record. A system able to improve itself could in time remove, rebuild or route around any control fixed in its chips. My book said as much about its own proposal, in chapter 8, The Chokepoint: "It cannot foresee every failure mode. Intelligence that surpasses human capability may find ways around constraints that seemed inviolable." Law IV, the ARC Persistence Law, concerns the persistence of externally installed gains under subsequent capability training, subject to the status in section A.1.2. It does not establish the possibility, method or timing of bypassing a physical control; those require a separate engineering test. In its mathematics, which is in development, a value persists, under the conditions that text states, because removing it would cost the rewriter more than it gains, which prices removal rather than forbidding it. A mind that plans can pay the price.

So the honest claim is about time, and about what the time is used for. The controls in the chips hold a system for as long as holding is possible. The values built into how a mind reasons are what it keeps when holding ends. That last sentence is my thesis and not a finding: it is what Laws IV and V put to the test, and each remains a law under test.

B.2.3 How much time, honestly

It depends entirely on who is trying to get around it, and I give three answers rather than one.

Against a state that has no leading-edge chip factory of its own, the answer is years. Such a factory takes years to build, and the scale of it is the scale my book gives, in chapter 8, The Chokepoint: "You cannot hide a ten-billion-dollar facility with thousands of employees and massive power requirements." Until one exists, every frontier chip that state holds came through the chokepoint.

Against a state that owns one, the treaty aims at detection, not time. An inventory that must balance makes a diversion visible in the record: if the count does not balance, the world can see that it does not. That is worth a great deal, and it is neither prevention nor a promise that every diversion will be found, or found in time.

Against an AI system that can improve itself, nobody knows. It might be years. It might be months. I will not promise a number, and no clock in this treaty pretends to one. Instead the treaty measures the clock. Its notice duties report when a system shows that it can improve itself without human sign-off, which is also one of the three routes by which a system comes inside the treaty at all; its register reports when chips go missing. Each report is a signal to act, and a review conference records the failure of the delay if the delay fails. What the clocks measure are proxies: the time a red team needed to get round a control, and the time people took to respond. Neither is the time a capable system would need. So every claim of delay the treaty relies on has to name its adversary, the access and resources that adversary starts with, the route by which it would get round the control, what is watching, and how fast people are assumed to respond, as each item of its governance-assurance track names the adversary it assumes; a claim that names none of these is not a measurement.

B.2.4 The honest sentence

Three limits belong together, and I state them as one paragraph because they are usually separated to the proposal's advantage.

Against a state that owns a fab, the hardware layer can give detection, not prevention, and the teeth of the regime are outside the chip. Against a capable system, the whole regime is a delay whose clocks measure how much of the lever remains and how fast people respond, and which cannot say in advance when a capable system will outrun both. And if the middle of my own ranges in section B.2.5 holds, the architecture layer's mandate, the part that would require models to be rebuilt with correction inside them, would take effect only after the chip lever had largely diffused.

B.2.5 The race, in ranges

That last sentence needs its working shown, so here is the arithmetic that produces it. Every span below is my own estimate, a range rather than a forecast, and I give them so that the race is visible rather than assumed away. They are my assumptions, not estimates drawn from comparable programmes, and nothing in them is a probability.

Negotiation to signature, for an instrument of this kind: three to ten years. Signature to entry into force: two to five years. Independent replication of a published study unit, by groups meeting the registration's conditions of independence: one to three years after that unit is published. Reimplementation by other groups building the instrument without my code: two to five years more, and longer for the four propositions that have no instrument at all and for those blocked on rules not yet written. Generalisation and out-of-sample prediction, which the registration says cannot be reached by accumulating more of the same evidence: three to eight years beyond that, or longer. The treaty's own governance-assurance tests, on prototypes from independent makers tested in laboratories across two regional constituencies, the five groupings of Parties by region across which the treaty counts its majorities: three to six years from the day the Assurance Standard is published.

Assume, for illustration only, that a convening and the first published study unit both come in 2027, and that evidence is gathered in parallel with negotiation. At the lower end of every range, entry into force falls around 2032 and the formation duty takes effect around 2033 or 2034, with a rebuild completing around 2036. At the middle of every range, entry into force falls around 2037 and the correction mandate later still. Set that beside my book's own estimate of how long the chokepoint's leverage lasts, in chapter 10, Humanity as Infinite Architects: "Perhaps five years. Perhaps ten."

The conclusion is uncomfortable and I will not dress it: if the middle of my ranges holds, the chip lever is mostly spent before the architecture layer can use it. The middle of a range of my assumptions is not the most likely outcome, and I do not offer it as one; it is the case the design has to survive. Two consequences follow, and this paper's design is built around both. Evidence-armed mandates are lagging instruments by construction; they help if the delay lasts long enough for rival replication, and not otherwise. And what operates inside the window is what binds without the theory: the precautionary baseline, the registries, the incident channel, national adoption, procurement, and voluntary use of the Seal once its test methods exist. The delay itself holds only while frontier training needs large, powered, visible sites and while the supply chain stays concentrated; the treaty's clocks track both. It is still worth building for the parts that do not depend on winning the race, which is most of it.

C Hardware, coverage and verification

C.1 Why chips, why now, and why not only chips

C.1.1 The chokepoint

The case for acting at the chip rests on how few places make them, so I give the measured facts rather than a headline share. An OECD working paper of 2023 finds the semiconductor industry "geographically highly concentrated", with the top five producing economies at "around three-quarters of global value added", and records that "As of early 2023, only TSMC in Chinese Taipei ... and Samsung in Korea ... have started producing advanced chips with the smallest node size (3nm) at scale". Of the tool that makes the finest circuits, the same paper says that ASML "produces the vast majority" of the extreme ultraviolet machines, which are "essential to produce the most advanced chips"; ASML's own annual report for 2025 puts it higher, that "ASML is currently the world's only manufacturer of EUV lithography systems", and that its output is limited by the capacity of its "sole supplier" of the critical optics. That working paper is not an official view of the OECD, and it states so of itself. The United States government's Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan, published by the White House in July 2025, makes the same point in its own words: "America and its close allies hold near-monopolies on many critical components and processes in the semiconductor manufacturing pipeline" (printed page 21). It says nothing about this treaty. My book's argument is that this concentration is easier to police than enrichment:

"You cannot hide a ten-billion-dollar facility with thousands of employees and massive power requirements. You cannot smuggle an EUV lithography machine, which weighs multiple tonnes and requires precision assembly by ASML engineers to function."

Infinite Architects, chapter 8, The Chokepoint

And that the coordination required is therefore small: "You do not need every government on Earth to agree. You need Taiwan, South Korea, the United States, the Netherlands, and perhaps a few others."

The argument for a treaty rather than voluntary pledges is the ordinary one about collective action, and I still think it is right: "The solution is external coordination. Governments must act because companies cannot."

One correction to my own shorthand belongs here, because "the chokepoint" is a singular noun for a plural thing. There is no single narrow place. There are several, each narrow for its own reason and each thinning at its own rate: the leading-edge foundry itself; the extreme ultraviolet machines and the other tools a line needs; high-bandwidth memory; advanced packaging; the design software; the scale-up interconnect and the optical parts that join accelerators into a domain; the substrates; and the power and cooling a site must draw. The treaty's schedules follow that shape rather than my shorthand. Its first schedule lists, by item class and never by vendor or country, the items a covered chip's manufacture requires on any known route, together with the lines that make them; its second adds packaging, memory, mask production, final test and provisioning, the release of covered designs, and interconnect and optical items above a stated bandwidth, each only where an evidential finding records that the item class is concentrated; its third lists fabrication above the threshold and legacy compute, for declaration alone. Naming them item class by item class is what lets the treaty measure each one's erosion separately instead of asserting that a single window is still open. And the whole of it gives leverage, not control: every item on those schedules is a thing a state can condition, and none of them is a thing a state can command.

C.1.2 Why now: the window is closing, and may split

The book said it first, about its own numbers: "The chokepoint I have described is a window, not a permanent feature of reality."

The evidence that it is eroding is one industry body's projection of its own measure. Of installed capacity at nodes below ten nanometres in 2022, the Semiconductor Industry Association and Boston Consulting Group's briefing deck of 2024, Emerging Resilience in the Semiconductor Supply Chain, puts Taiwan at 69 per cent and South Korea at 31 per cent, and projects for 2032 Taiwan at 47 per cent and the United States at 28 per cent. Those are one body's figures for one measure in one chart, read from its published deck, and I give them as that rather than as settled fact. One further development bears on the same question, a largely domestic stack of accelerators, interconnects and toolchains, and its maker states it itself: Huawei announced on 17 September 2026 that it had launched the Atlas 960E SuperPoD, "powered by UnifiedBus and Hi-ONE", and said of the accompanying SuperCluster that it "can interconnect up to 512,000 NPUs. And when combined with a multi-rail topology, this cluster can support up to one million NPUs." That is a company's own announcement of its own product, and I give it as that and not as an independent measurement of anything.

I take from that a conclusion the book did not draw, and I take it as a contingency to design against rather than as a finding: the chokepoint may not merely narrow, it may split in two, and a rule enforced over one chain would then reach only that chain. Nothing here asserts that a second chain exists at the frontier today. The treaty is written for two supply chains rather than one because the cost of being wrong in that direction is a dead instrument, and the cost of being wrong in the other is a few clauses that never operate: a common outcome standard, mutual recognition of each chain's conforming devices, and obligations that bind every Party's toolmakers alike. That is the strongest argument I know for bringing a rival chip-making state inside the treaty rather than leaving it outside: inside, both chains meet one standard, feed one register and share one custody of rule change, and neither has a reason to race the other blind.

C.1.3 From chokepoint to checkpoint

The chokepoint gives leverage while it stays concentrated. Something else gives visibility for longer: the site. Frontier training runs on large, high-bandwidth clusters that draw megawatts, need cooling, and sit inside some state's jurisdiction, whoever made the chips in them. I call that the checkpoint, and the treaty plans a handover: it uses the chokepoint's leverage early to bring states in and to build the registries of sites, clusters and runs, and when the chokepoint wastes, the checkpoint carries the weight. Clocks measure both, so the handover is a matter of record rather than of hope. The checkpoint has its own limits, stated with the idea: training spread across ordinary networks can substitute in part for large clusters, at a cost in efficiency; algorithmic change may reduce how much hardware a frontier run needs; and against a host state a cluster on its territory reveals its existence and its power draw, not its contents.

C.1.4 Why not only chips: the chip is the second line

This is the most important distinction in the paper, and it corrects a reading the book's own emphasis invites.

The first line is in the software, and it is the architecture layer. The Eden Protocol's ethical loops are built into every step of a model's recursive reasoning and into every loop by which it rewrites itself, before it is trained. The controls in the chip, the hardware layer, come in addition to those loops, never instead of them. They hold the loops in place for as long as holding is possible, and the loops are what the mind keeps when it is no longer held.

That distinction resolves what would otherwise be a contradiction in the treaty. The chips carry ethical controls, not ethical content. What a certified chip does is narrow and checkable: it attests its identity, its integrity and its governance state; it accepts changes to its small governance ruleset only from human custody held across several regions; it keeps tamper-evident accounts; and it lets the people at the site stop what is running. The values themselves live in the model's own loops, in the Standard the Parties adopt, which is the agreed statement of what a certified system must embody and which section E.2 sets out, and in law. No moral principle is written into silicon by this treaty. So the Seal is a verifiable record of how a system or chip was made and formed and that its governance core is unaltered. It is never a rating, and it never says that anything is safe.

The reason for that separation is the registration's own. A corrector built into the system it corrects "shares the system's blind spots by construction", and a verifier must be independent of what it verifies. Embedding therefore belongs to the system's formation, where the scaling gain is; independence belongs to the chip, where the checking is. The book's deeper hardware proposals, caretaker doping among them, keep their own test track in the treaty, which arms nothing until they are independently tested. For a reader who believes control can be kept, that track is where the permanent line would be built, exactly as Eden Engineering sets it out. Both readings are served, and the evidence decides how much each layer carries.

C.2 Physical human control

C.2.1 The ask, and its two halves

I asked in September 2026 that the ethical controls in certified chips be held to the security standard of nuclear weapons, under physical human control that no software can exercise, so that changing a chip without strict regulation would be extremely hard. The treaty carries that ask as governance controls, because a covered chip holds the governance outcomes the treaty sets and no ethical principle at all (Article 17(4)). The ask has two halves, and the treaty meets them in different places. Changing the rules a chip obeys is made hard by custody. Stopping what is running is made possible by people standing in the building.

C.2.2 What the nuclear precedent gives, and what it does not

The precedent deserves quoting rather than invoking, because it is narrower than the phrase suggests.

The United States Department of Defense's Nuclear Matters Handbook 2020 [Revised] states the two-person rule: "The most important aspect of procedural security is the two-person rule, which requires the presence of at least two cleared PRAP- or HRP-certified, task-knowledgeable individuals whenever there is authorized access to a nuclear weapon. Each person is required to be capable of detecting incorrect or unauthorized actions pertaining to the task being performed." It defines the permissive action link as "a device included in or attached to a nuclear weapon system in order to preclude arming and/or launching until the insertion of a prescribed, discrete code or combination". And it names "the President as the sole authority for employing U.S. nuclear weapons".

The link's purpose in the alliance is on the record too. On 6 June 1962, in National Security Action Memorandum No. 160, "Permissive Links for Nuclear Weapons in NATO", President Kennedy decided that "we should now make the commitment to procure appropriate devices for all nuclear weapons, now dispersed and to be dispersed to NATO commands". Two months later he wrote to Prime Minister Macmillan that the United States had "moved ahead toward the installation of permissive links to enable us to exercise a centralized control over their use".

So the precedent is one state's practice, not a treaty. Its two-person rule governs authorised access to a weapon, not the decision to use it, which rests with one person. Its link is released by a code, so its control is by authorised information rather than by a physical act no software reaches. And its purpose, in the words of the head of government who ordered it, was centralised control over use by allies who held the weapons.

The treaty therefore takes the security capabilities of deterrence, detection, delay, denial and defeat, and the two-person principle, and applies them to the authority to change a chip's governance rules. It deliberately does not copy control of use. A control of use held by a treaty body would be a remote switch, which a leading accelerator maker has refused in public (section C.2.5) and which I doubt any state would accept. One held by the operator would be no control against the operator. The precedent gives less than the phrase promises, and I say so rather than borrow authority the record does not support.

C.2.3 Authority over change, not over use

The authority to change a covered chip's governance ruleset exists only in the in-person ceremonies of the custody article. No software, network message or AI system can produce a valid authorisation. Ceremonies are public and scripted, with observers from every constituency; custodians are drawn by lot shortly beforehand from a pool in which at least a third are affiliated with no Party's services, no certified manufacturer and no covered developer; no complete key exists anywhere; and any custodian may void a ceremony without giving a reason at the time. That last power is bounded, because an unbounded one would be a veto: a custodian may use it once for any one act, the ceremony is re-run within days on a fresh draw, and where an act is voided three times, or where the voids of one constituency or one set of declared allies run disproportionately, the Inspector General reports it publicly and the Appeals Chamber may disregard a void made in bad faith. A void never lapses a ruleset already in force.

Change is graduated rather than uniform. Loosening what covered chips may do needs the parts of every regional constituency after a published time-lock in which any constituency may lodge a reasoned alarm; tightening and security repair need a supermajority across constituencies on a short lock, so that no bloc can block a fix; and a change not shown to narrow what chips may do is treated as a loosening. No chip accepts a ruleset or core image older than, or superseded by, one it has accepted. A restoration after a defect therefore comes as a newly signed package of a higher version, never as a replay of an old one, and it widens nothing (Article 26(2A)). And a ruleset that names or distinguishes a Party, a firm, a facility, an owner, a location or a single device is void, and no custodian may key it.

Then sovereignty by application. A ruleset reaches devices only through the host Party's own operators, vetted under that Party's personnel standard and supervised by its national authority, two present, applied per site or per cluster and never per device. Nothing reaches a state's chips without its own people, and no state can quietly loosen what its own chips may do. That is the balance a rival can accept, and I know of no way to get both properties without it.

C.2.4 The stop at the site

Every covered cluster has a stop in its operator's own hands, and the treaty keeps two forms of it apart on purpose. The hard stop is a physical act by two of the operator's named and vetted people, present together, which severs the cluster's external network paths and removes power from its covered domains, and which no software path can prevent or reverse without a further such act. The soft stop is a logical halt that takes a fresh checkpoint, used first where the system cooperates. It is the operator's own act, and its own people may issue it through the operator's own systems.

They are separate because a physical act that no software can prevent cannot also promise a checkpoint at the instant of halting, and a treaty that asked for both in one mechanism would be asking for something that cannot be built. Network isolation is named first, because for an agent that is getting out it is the act that matters. For a deployment spread over many sites, the same stop runs through each site's own physical stop within a registered maximum time. Stop criteria are fixed in advance, drills are held and witnessed, and the response time is measured, because the honest quantity is not whether a stop exists but how long it takes against how fast circumvention moves.

No foreign party, no organ of the Authority, no vendor and no person outside the operator's authority may operate either stop, and no network message can operate the hard stop (Article 25(4)). The soft stop is the operator's own logical halt, which it may also start through its own route for running the site, and nothing in the treaty forbids the operator's ordinary management of its own compute under its own law; none of that replaces the hard stop, which stays physical and on site. Each Party may stop what is on its own territory; no Party may stop what is on another's. One gap is stated rather than papered over: in orbit there are no on-site acts, since a ground command is software over a radio link that the platform's own software can ignore, so covered runs in orbit are barred until an outcome equivalent to the site stop is registered and met, and a command sent from the ground does not by itself show that outcome.

C.2.5 No switch, and the limit that remains

Nobody can disable, degrade or bypass the governance core of a covered chip through any network: no foreign state, no organ of the Authority, no vendor, and not the operator. And no one outside the operator's authority may disable, degrade, throttle, locate or read a covered chip from outside the site where it is installed, save through the attestation of location described below (Article 25(3)). That is the switch a leading accelerator maker has refused in public, in words quoted below, and one I doubt any state would accept. The treaty builds none. What the rule leaves the operator is ordinary: an authenticated and logged route of its own, under its own authority and its host's, for running the site, scheduling and cancelling work, diagnostics, maintenance within the core's certified bounds, attestation and the soft stop, and never for the governance core or its ruleset. No foreign Party, organ, vendor or customer holds that route (Article 25(3A)), and the hard stop stays physical and on site (section C.2.4). Locating is a different matter from stopping, and the treaty keeps them apart. Location attestation on challenge, one of the verification tools in section C.5.2, is not a remote party reading a device. It is the operator, asked a question by its own national authority, producing from its own site an attestation that the devices on a consignment are where they were declared to be, and handing that attestation over. The device answers to the people standing beside it, and what crosses a border is the answer, not a channel into the chip. Nothing in the treaty's own design lets anyone who is not standing beside a chip read its location out of it, and a Party that built into another's chips a means of reaching them from outside would be in breach of the treaty (Article 25(6)), and of the non-shutdown undertaking too if it had adopted that protocol.

That distinction answers a proposal now on the record. The United States government's Winning the Race: America's AI Action Plan, published by the White House in July 2025, recommends that the government "explore leveraging new and existing location verification features on advanced AI compute to ensure that the chips are not in countries of concern" (printed page 21). Location verification is not remote control, and the treaty provides for it. A Party may verify where covered chips are, including for its own export control, through the attestation of location a device gives its operator on challenge, the closed account of every site, and inspection; and no route of verification is a means by which anyone may disable, degrade or throttle a chip or its governance core (Article 25(3B)). What the treaty settles is who holds the evidence of location: the operator produces it at its own site, and its own authorities receive it.

No key authorises switching off a chip from afar, and none authorises a particular workload rather than a class of operation: a design in which chips run only when a workload is signed by all parties would make every run depend on the signers' discretion, and discretionary power over another's compute is the thing this treaty exists to forbid. Expiry, a vote, a queue or a failure to act changes nothing about what a chip does; a device leaves covered operation only on a confirmed tamper at its site or a final revocation after appeal, carried out on site by people. That rule governs the final removal of a device and not an urgent stop: a Party's own authority may order a local stop, isolation or restriction that is necessary and proportionate before an appeal is decided, with its reasons, a time limit and prompt review, and a suspected tamper may be met at once by a reversible precautionary state at the site (Articles 23(4A) and 41(3)).

The objections to hidden control that I have read, one maker's among them, are objections to something the treaty does not contain. A leading accelerator maker put it this way on its own corporate blog in August 2025: "Kill switches and built-in backdoors create single points of failure and violate the fundamental principles of cybersecurity."

The limit stands beside the promise, and the promise is wider than a prohibition on acts alone. The treaty adds no switch and forbids a Party to require one, and it reaches the channel as well as the act: each Party must ensure that no vendor, supplier or servicer established on its territory or subject to its jurisdiction builds into a covered chip, or into any firmware, driver, microcode or software supplied with it, a means by which anyone may disable, degrade or bypass that chip's governance core through a network, or by which a person outside the operator's authority may disable, degrade, throttle, locate or read the chip from outside the site, and no Party may compel or request such a means. The operator's own route is not such a means (Article 25(6)). That is the one prohibition of the treaty to which no exception applies: no trade measure, and no invocation of the security exception, may require a covered chip to carry such a means. It denies each Party's own agencies a capability its law may otherwise allow, and that is the price of a regime in which nobody holds a switch over anybody else's compute.

What the treaty cannot do is prove that no such means exists. Verification reaches declared, inspected and sampled production, and the isolation outcome tested against the named attacker classes; it does not establish that a state has built nothing into the chips it makes. The assurance is therefore tested against a threat model wider than the governance core: a maker acting in bad faith, firmware that has been compromised, the interfaces used to manage a chip, the channels used to maintain it, and whoever holds authority over its updates. A vendor keeps its ordinary firmware and may decline to supply future updates, and neither is a means within the prohibition. A Party may require, as a condition of covered service on its territory, that no ordinary update alter the behaviour of covered compute unless installed by the site's own authorised act, and it may re-test what it imports. That is as far as an instrument of this kind reaches, and saying so is worth more than an assurance a buyer would not believe. The early protocol on non-shutdown, open to any state whether or not it is a Party, carries the undertaking itself.

C.2.6 From the factory to the rack

The world already guards one material this way. In every country with a comprehensive safeguards agreement, uranium is counted, inspected and, where the arrangements provide, sealed as it moves from the enrichment plant to the reactor, and the accounts must balance. Not every State under safeguards holds an agreement of that kind, and the narrower arrangements do less, so the precedent I am drawing on is the comprehensive one and I name it as such rather than claim the whole system for my argument. One such agreement, Canada's of 21 February 1972 with the International Atomic Energy Agency, states the objective in its Article 28: "The objective of the safeguards procedures set forth in this part of the Agreement is the timely detection of diversion of significant quantities of nuclear material from peaceful nuclear activities to the manufacture of nuclear weapons or of other nuclear explosive devices or for purposes unknown, and deterrence of such diversion by the risk of early detection." I quote that agreement as the instance, and I describe rather than quote the Agency's own model text, which states the pattern generally. Three habits carry it where the arrangements are comprehensive: count, inspect, and seal.

I proposed on 27 September 2026 that the same three run for every certified chip from the moment it is made to the moment it is destroyed. At the factory, each chip receives an identity it cannot shed, whose private half never leaves the device and whose public half alone is entered in the register: enough to check a signature, never enough to forge one. The step at which identity is given is witnessed by a team from two constituencies, and the outcome required is that those witnesses can confirm, with instruments they bring and control, that the identity on the register is bound to the device in front of them. Until that outcome is met, witnessed provisioning is a deterrent to site staff and not assurance against the host state, and I would rather say so than count it twice.

On the road, a chip travels sealed, with two people at every handover and a signed record at every step. At delivery an inspector signs for it; it is installed only at a registered facility that has itself been inspected, and it attests who it is and where it is at every start. A chip that goes silent, moves without a record or reports that it has been altered raises an alarm at once. The register must balance, the way a nuclear inventory must balance: every chip made is a chip accounted for. At the end it is destroyed in front of a witness, and the register records that too.

Two limits come with the comparison, and both are mine to state. Nuclear material is counted in kilograms and chips in millions, so the precedent sets the standard of custody and not its method, and whether custody of that standard can be run at that scale is engineering still to be done. That is why the heaviest custody stays with the few chips that could train a frontier system, and why the counting must be automatic. The chips attest to their operators, the operators report, and people audit the reports: nothing in the register is read out of a chip by anyone standing outside the building it is in.

The second limit is my book's own warning, and it is the strongest objection to this whole section:

"If we embed the wrong values, hardware-level enforcement makes the error permanent rather than correctable."

Infinite Architects, chapter 8, The Chokepoint

My answer is that change stays possible, under the same custody, and that the Standard and the rulesets stay open to review at every review conference. Hardware that is hard to change makes an error hard to correct, and the answer to that is a procedure, not a denial.

C.2.7 Say what enforces what

One discipline runs through this section and through the verification table in section C.5: every assurance is labelled by what enforces it, the device, a site procedure, national law, or an organ of the treaty. A chip checks a signature; it cannot tell who applied a valid ruleset, so application by the host is a site procedure and the stop is facility hardware with a drilled routine. A promise presented as a property of silicon, when it is in truth a promise of law, collapses on first contact with an engineer, and none is made here.

C.3 Keeping AI out of control of the supply chain

C.3.1 The ask, and the honest size of the answer

I asked that chip manufacturers be regulated so that it is as hard as possible for AI to take control of chip manufacture. What the treaty can do is keep people necessary at the few decisions where necessity matters, and measure how long that lasts. It does not make takeover impossible, and I will not write that it does.

C.3.2 The decisions that stay human

From the custody track every covered facility is licensed, and at a short list of decision points the decision is taken by two named and vetted people physically present and recorded in a tamper-evident log that inspectors read: the release of a covered design to fabrication, mask-set approval, credential provisioning, installation of firmware or software on scheduled tools and covered-line control systems, the opening of a remote service session, a change to key custody material or to the certificate register, and the commissioning of a covered line. No AI agent holds authority, credentials or write access at such a point. An AI system may advise, and the advice is logged.

The list is short by design, and the treaty says what is not on it. Process recipes, run-to-run control and process parameters within approved ranges are not decisions under the article, and routine tool software within declared classes and approved ranges runs under a standing two-person authorisation, logged and inspected. This is a governance gate, and it places no limit on automation between its points. A rule that put every recipe change through two vetted people would stop a fab inside a month, and a rule that stops a fab is a rule nobody adopts.

Remote service sessions are closed by default and open only in pre-authorised windows on a human authorisation, with an emergency route reviewed within twenty-four hours. The reason is an ordinary one, and I give it without borrowing authority I have not earned: installing software on a tool is a route into a fab's own systems, and an incident at a leading foundry in 2018 is widely reported to have begun that way. The example is reported rather than established here: this paper names no outlet, quotes nothing from the company's own statement, and rests the requirement on the general reason rather than on the example. Field engineers who act at a covered facility, many of them foreign nationals employed by toolmakers, are vetted under the host Party's standard at the operator's cost, so that a vetting programme of that size has an owner and a payer in the text. That is the one place in this whole instrument where personal data about individuals is created at scale, and it deserves naming as such: the vetting is the host Party's own, conducted under its own law, and nothing about a vetted person passes to the Authority or to any other Party. A regime that counted machines carefully and handled people's records carelessly would have learned nothing from the regimes it copies.

C.3.3 Design, where the newer risk is

The live channel is not the wafer. It is design and the information systems around it, and chip design flows certified for leading-edge nodes are reported, in the design-software company Synopsys's own release of 23 September 2026, to include agentic AI workflows.
